Frobenius-Ehresmann structures and Cartan geometries in positive characteristic
Yasuhiro Wakabayashi

Abstract
. The aim of the present paper is to lay the foundation for a theory of Ehresmann structures in positive characteristic, generalizing the Frobenius-projective and Frobenius-affine structures defined in the previous work. This theory deals with atlases of étale coordinate charts on varieties modeled on homogenous spaces of algebraic groups, which we call Frobenius-Ehresmann structures. These structures are compared with Cartan geometries in positive characteristic, as well as with higher-dimensional generalizations of dormant indigenous bundles. In particular, we investigate the conditions under which these geometric structures are equivalent to each other. Also, we consider the classification problem of Frobenius-Ehresmann structures on algebraic curves. The latter half of the present paper discusses the deformation theory of indigenous bundles in the algebraic setting. The tangent and obstruction spaces of various deformation functors are computed in terms of the hypercohomology groups of certain complexes. As a consequence, we formulate and prove the Ehresmann-Weil-Thurston principle for Frobenius-Ehresmann structures. This fact asserts that deformations of a variety equipped with a Frobenius-Ehresmann structure are completely determined by their monodromy crystals.

Key words: Frobenius-Ehresmann structure, Frobenius-projective structure, positive characteristic, Klein geometry, Cartan geometry, indigenous bundle, Ehresmann-Weil-Thurston principle, deformation theory, differential operator, crystal, connection, homogenous space,
p
-curvature

1.
Introduction

1.1.
Review of Ehresmann structures and Cartan geometries

In the spirit of F. Klein’s Erlangen program of 1872, the essence of classical geometry is the idea
that

geometry
is the study of properties on homogenous spaces that are invariant under the action of transformations.
This idea includes, for instance, Euclidean geometry
by considering the isometry group on Euclidean space.
On the basis of this idea and those of H. Poincaré, E. Cartan, and other mathematicians, C. Ehresmann
(cf.
[
13
]
) initiated a general study of geometric structures, which was later
promoted by W. P. Thurston (cf.
[
50
]
).
For a homogenous space
P
obtained as the quotient of a finite-dimensional Lie group
G
,
we consider
a maximal atlas of coordinate charts on a manifold into
P
such that
on any two overlapping coordinate patches, the change of coordinates is given by elements of

G
;
this geometric structure is called an
Ehresmann
(G,P)
-structure
(following
[
19
]
and
[
11
]
), or simply a
(G,P)
-structure
.
This notion allows us to
investigate various geometries in the unifying frame of the model geometry
(G,P)
.

A basic example in the holomorphic setting is given by
the complex projective line
\mathbb{P}^{1}_{\mathbb{C}}
considered as
a homogenous space for
the Möbius group
\mathrm{PGL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.
A
(\mathrm{PGL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}),\mathbb{P}^{1}_{\mathbb{C}})
-structure on a Riemann surface is known as a
(complex) projective structure
, and it
appears in the study of second-order linear differential equations.
Also, flat
\mathbb{P}^{1}_{\mathbb{C}}
-bundles corresponding, via the Riemann-Hilbert correspondence, to projective structures and their moduli spaces have been studied under the name of
indigenous bundles
or
\mathrm{PGL}_{2}
-opers
(cf., e.g.,
[
1
]
,
[
17
]
,
[
21
]
).
They play a major role in understanding the framework of the uniformization theorem.
As an important consequence of the uniformization theorem for Riemann surfaces,
each Riemann surface has a canonical projective structure that is obtained by collecting various local inverses of a universal covering map.
In the hyperbolic case, the monodromy of this projective structure defines a Fuchsian subgroup of
\mathrm{PSL}(\mathbb{R})
, which
reflects the geometry of the underlying Riemann surface from the group-theoretic viewpoint.

The monodromy of projective structures can be generalized to a general
(G,P)
, as above.
Denote by
\mathrm{Hom}(\pi_{1}(X),G)/G
the representation variety
classifying homomorphisms
\pi_{1}(X)\rightarrow G
(up to an inner automorphism) of the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(X)
of
X
based at a fixed point.
It may also be identified with the moduli space of
G
-valued local systems on
X
.
Although there are numerous special cases (cf.
[
2
]
,
[
22
]
,
[
56
]
,
[
57
]
,
[
58
]
),
Thurston
provided a guiding principle for classifying
(G,P)
-structures by using
\mathrm{Hom}(\pi_{1}(X),G)/G
(cf.
[
50
, § 5.3.1]
).
This so-called
Ehresmann-Weil-Thurston principle
ensures that the map associating to
each
(G,P)
-structure its monodromy representation
\pi_{1}(X)\rightarrow G

gives a local homeomorphism between the deformation space of
(G,P)
-structures
and
\mathrm{Hom}(\pi_{1}(X),G)/G
(cf.
[
10
]
,
[
19
]
,
[
34
]
).

Also, Cartan generalized
Klein’s homogeneous model spaces to the corresponding infinitesimal notion (in exactly the same way as Riemannian geometry is the infinitesimal version of Euclidean geometry), namely
Cartan geometries
.
Cartan geometries can be described by using higher-dimensional generalizations of indigenous bundles (=
\mathrm{PGL}_{2}
-opers), which are principal bundles equipped with a connection satisfying a certain transversality condition.
A fundamental result in that theory is that a Cartan geometry, or the corresponding indigenous bundle,
admits a curvature tensor that is zero exactly when it comes from an Ehresmann structure.
See
[
47
]
for a modern treatment of this theory.

1.2.
Previous work related to Frobenius-projective structures

Then, is it possible to develop the geometry of Ehresmann structures and their generalizations by Cartan
in positive characteristic
?
On the one hand,
Cartan geometries and the corresponding indigenous bundles can be defined in any characteristic
because of the algebraic nature of their formulations.
On the other hand,
due to its analytic formulation,
the definition of an Ehresmann structure cannot be adopted, at least in positive characteristic, as it is.
Indeed, if we had defined it in a naive fashion, it would be nothing other than a trivial example of a variety having such a structure.
By taking this unfortunate fact into account, we need to consider appropriate replacements.
Regarding this matter, Y. Hoshi (cf.
[
23
]
) introduced
Frobenius-projective structures
(of finite level) to deal with analogues of complex projective structures on a Riemann surface.
(He also introduced in
[
24
]
the notion of a
Frobenius-affine structure
as an analogue of an affine structure on a Riemann surface.)
Frobenius-projective structures have provided a rich and deep story under the identifications with various
equivalent realizations (in the case of
N=1
), e.g.,
dormant indigenous bundles
(=
dormant
\mathrm{PGL}_{2}
-opers
) and projective connections with a full set of solutions.
See
[
40
]
,
[
52
]
, and
[
54
]
for the study of
these objects and their moduli space
in the context of
p
-adic Teichmüller theory developed by S. Mochizuki.

Also, in
[
53
]
, the author generalized Frobenius-projective structures to higher-dimensional varieties (i.e., generalized the local model to the
n
-dimensional projective space
\mathbb{P}^{n}
equipped with the natural
\mathrm{PGL}_{n+1}
-action for an arbitrary
n
) including the case of infinite level.
The main subject of
loc. cit.
is the classification problem, first studied by S. Kobayashi and T. Ochiai (cf.
[
29
]
,
[
30
]
) for complex manifolds, of varieties admitting such structures.
For example,
we proved
the positive characteristic version of Gunning’s formula (cf.
[
53
, Theorem B]
), which gives a fundamental criterion
for the existence of Frobenius-projective structures on a variety.
Various other results proved there are compared with the classical facts in the holomorphic setting; these comparisons show that
Frobenius-projective structures
should be appropriate replacements for complex projective structures.
The present work is, in some sense, a continuation and generalization of those previous studies.
In particular, it includes a very general construction of the correspondence
between Frobenius-projective structures and dormant indigenous bundles.

1.3.
Frobenius-Ehresmann structures and Cartan geometries in char.
\,p>0

The aim of the present paper is
to lay
the foundation of
a theory of Ehresmann structures and Cartan geometries in positive characteristic for arbitrary local models not just
\mathrm{PGL}_{n+1}
.
Let
k
be an algebraically close field of characteristic
p>2
and

P

a smooth homogeneous space over
k
obtained as the quotient of a connected smooth algebraic group
G
by a closed subgroup
H
.
Also, let
f:X\rightarrow S
be a smooth morphism of schemes over
k
.
For a positive integer
N
, denote by
X^{(N)}
the

N
-th relative Frobenius twist of
X
over
S
(cf. §
2.1
), i.e., the base-change of
X
via the
N
-th iterate of the Frobenius endomorphism
F_{S}
of
S
.
The sheaf
G_{X/S}^{(N)}
on
X^{(N)}
represented by
G
can be regarded as a sheaf on
X
via the underlying homeomorphism of the
N
-th relative Frobenius morphism
F^{(N)}_{X/S}:X\rightarrow X^{(N)}
.
Then, we define a
Frobenius-Ehresmann
(G,P)
-structure of level
N
, or an
F^{N}
-
(G,P)
-structure
for short, on
X/S

as a maximal collection
\mathcal{S}^{\heartsuit}
of étale coordinate charts on
X
forming a left
G_{X/S}^{(N)}
-torsor (cf. Definition
2.2.2
for the precise definition).
The local sections of
G^{(N)}_{X/S}
acting on
\mathcal{S}^{\heartsuit}

play the same role as
the coordinate changes appearing in the classical definition of an Ehresmann
(G,P)
-structure.
Moreover,
a compatible collection of
F^{N}
-
(G,P)
-structures for various
N
is called an
F^{\infty}
-
(G,P)
-structure
(cf. Definition
2.3.1
).
For each
N\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\sqcup\{\infty\}
, we shall write

(1)

\displaystyle F^{N}\text{-}\mathrm{Ehr}_{(G,P),X/S}

(cf. (
32
), (
36
)) for the set of
F^{N}
-
(G,P)
-structures on
X/S
.
To simplify the notation slightly, we will write
F\text{-}\mathrm{Ehr}_{(G,P),X/S}:=F^{1}\text{-}\mathrm{Ehr}_{(G,P),X/S}
, and refer to any
F^{1}
-
(G,P)
-structure as an
F
-
(G,P)
-structure
.
Truncations to lower levels via Frobenius pull-back (cf. (
33
)) give rise to a sequence of sets

(2)

\displaystyle F^{\infty}\text{-}\mathrm{Ehr}_{(G,P),X/S}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow F^{N}\text{-}\mathrm{Ehr}_{(G,P),X/S}\rightarrow\cdots F^{2}\text{-}\mathrm{Ehr}_{(G,P),X/S}\rightarrow F\text{-}\mathrm{Ehr}_{(G,P),X/S}.

Frobenius-Ehresmann structures, i.e., elements of sets in this sequence, are the central characters of our discussion, and they are specialized to Frobenius-projective structures if

G=\mathrm{PGL}_{n+1}
(
n>0
) and
H
is the subgroup
\mathrm{PGL}_{n+1}^{\circledcirc}
(cf. (
41
)) of
\mathrm{PGL}_{n+1}
consisting of elements stabilizing the point
[1:0:0:\cdots:0]
under an identification with the natural actions on the projective space
\mathbb{P}^{n}
.
Some of the fundamental subjects in the theory are as follows:

(i)

Compare Frobenius-Ehresmann structures with Cartan geometries in positive characteristic in analogy with the classical correspondence between Ehresmann structures and flat Cartan geometries;

(ii)

Describe which model geometries
(G,P)
a smooth scheme
X/S
may have locally, and up to what level
N
it may have an
F^{N}
-
(G,P)
-structure;

(iii)

For a fixed model
(G,P)
and
N\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\sqcup\{\infty\}
, describe all possible
F^{N}
-
(G,P)
-structures on
X/S
and all possible deformations of a fixed
F^{N}
-
(G,P)
-structure.

First, let us consider the relationship with Cartan geometries, i.e., (i) above.
We shall write
\mathfrak{g}:=\mathrm{Lie}(G)
, i.e., the Lie algebra of
G
.
Then, just as in the classical case,
we can define a
Cartan geometry on a scheme in positive characteristic.
A
Cartan geometry
with model
(G,H)
on
X/S
(cf. Definition
3.4.1
for the precise definition) is a pair

(3)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\clubsuit}:=(\mathcal{E}_{H},\omega)

consisting of an
H
-bundle
\mathcal{E}_{H}
on
X
and a
\mathfrak{g}
-valued
1
-form
\omega
on
\mathcal{E}_{H}/S
and
satisfying the following conditions:

•

The
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{E}_{H}}
-linear morphism
\omega^{\triangleright}:\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{E}_{H}/S}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{E}_{H}}\otimes_{k}\mathfrak{g}
corresponding to
\omega

is an isomorphism and
H
-equivariant with
H
acting on
\mathfrak{g}
via conjugation;

•

The restriction of
\omega
to each fiber of the projection
\mathcal{E}_{H}\rightarrow X
coincides with the Maurer-Cartan form
\omega_{H}
on
H
.

We say that the Cartan geometry
\mathcal{E}^{\clubsuit}
is
flat
if the curvature
\psi_{\omega}:=d\omega+\frac{1}{2}\cdot[\omega,\omega]
of
\omega
(cf. (
78
)) vanishes identically.
The standard example of a flat Cartan geometry is
defined on the homogeneous space
P
; it consists of
the natural projection
G\twoheadrightarrow P
, considered as an
H
-bundle on
P
, together with the Maurer-Cartan form
\omega_{G}
on
G
.
More generally,
given an
F
-
(G,P)
-structure
\mathcal{S}^{\heartsuit}
on
X/S
, we can glue together
copies of the standard one
(G\twoheadrightarrow P,\omega_{G})
defined on various
étale charts defining
\mathcal{S}^{\heartsuit}
and obtain a flat Cartan geometry
\mathcal{S}^{\heartsuit\Rightarrow\clubsuit}
.
The resulting assignment
\mathcal{S}^{\heartsuit}\mapsto\mathcal{S}^{\heartsuit\Rightarrow\clubsuit}

gives a map from
F\text{-}\mathrm{Ehr}_{(G,P),X/S}
to the set of (isomorphism classes of) flat Cartan geometries with model
(G,H)
on
X/S
.
Unlike the classical case, this map is not surjective, meaning that
a flat Cartan geometry does not necessarily come from an
F
-
(G,P)
-structure.
Then,
by what conditions on Cartan geometries can the image of this map be characterized?
The essential ingredient in understanding this matter is the
p
-curvature
{{}^{p}}\psi_{\omega}
of
\mathfrak{g}
-valued
1
-forms
\omega
introduced in Definition
3.7.2
.
If
\mathcal{E}^{\clubsuit}
is a flat Cartan geometry as above, then

{{}^{p}}\psi_{\omega}
is defined as the global section of
F_{X}^{*}(\Omega_{X/S}\otimes_{k}\mathfrak{g})
determined by

(4)

\displaystyle\langle F_{X}^{-1}(\partial),{{}^{p}}\psi_{\omega}\rangle=\omega^{\triangleright}(\partial)^{[p]}-\omega^{\triangleright}(\partial^{[p]})+\sum_{i=1}^{p-1}\frac{s_{i}(\partial,\omega^{\triangleright}(\partial))}{i}

for each local section
\partial\in\mathcal{T}_{X/S}
, where
(-)^{[\partial]}
denotes
p
-power operations and the
s_{i}
’s are defined at the beginning of §
3.7
.
We shall say that
\mathcal{E}^{\clubsuit}
is
p
-flat
if
{{}^{p}}\psi_{\omega}
vanishes identically.

Also, we will introduce a
dormant indigenous
(G,H)
-bundle
(cf. Definitions
4.1.4
and
4.4.1
), which generalizes the classical notion of a dormant indigenous bundle (= dormant
\mathrm{PGL}_{2}
-oper) on an algebraic curve; this idea is, roughly speaking, an
H
-bundle
\mathcal{E}_{H}
together with an
S
-connection
\nabla
on the induced
G
-bundle
\mathcal{E}_{G}:=\mathcal{E}_{H}\times^{H}G
such that
\mathcal{E}_{H}
is transverse to the horizontal distribution with respect to
\nabla
.
If
(G,H)=(\mathrm{PGL}_{n+1},\mathrm{PGL}_{n+1}^{\circledcirc})
, it is as formulated in
[
51
, § 1.7, Definition 1.7.1]
, where it is called an
F^{1}
-indigenous structure.
We prove (cf. Proposition
4.4.2
) that
giving a
p
-flat Cartan geometry with model
(G,H)
is equivalent to giving a dormant indigenous
(G,H)
-bundle.
Also, one of the fundamental results of the present paper is
that
the map

\mathcal{S}^{\heartsuit}\mapsto\mathcal{S}^{\heartsuit\Rightarrow\clubsuit}

mentioned above is
restricted to a bijective correspondence
between
F
-
(G,P)
-structures and
p
-flat Cartan geometries with model
(G,H)
, or equivalently, dormant indigenous
(G,H)
-bundles (cf. Theorem
3.9.3
).

We shall denote by

(5)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Car}^{p\text{-}\mathrm{flat}}_{(G,H),X/S}\left(\text{resp.,}\ \mathrm{Ind}^{{}^{\mathrm{Zzz...}}}_{(G,H),X/S}\right)

(cf. (
139
), (
230
)) the set of isomorphism classes of
p
-flat Cartan geometries with model
(G,H)
(resp., dormant indigenous
(G,H)
-bundles) on
X/S
.
Then,
what we claimed above can be summarized below;
this assertion is a positive characteristic version of Ehresmann’s results in
[
13
]
(cf.
[
8
, Theorem 2.2]
).

Theorem A

(cf. Theorem
3.9.3
, Proposition
4.4.2
)
.

Suppose that
G
is affine.
Then, there exists the following commutative diagram in which all the maps of sets are bijective:

Moreover, the formations of these bijections commute with pull-back to étale
X
-schemes, as well as with base-change to
S
-schemes.

1.4.
Generalizations to higher levels

The next topic of the present paper will be to extend
the bijections in the above theorem to higher level.
To do this,
we equip a Cartan geometry
\mathcal{E}^{\clubsuit}:=(\mathcal{E}_{H},\omega)
with an additional choice of
a
G
-bundle
\mathcal{G}
on
X^{(N)}
together with an isomorphism
\left(\mathcal{E}_{H}\times^{H}G=:\right)\mathcal{E}_{G}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\rightarrow}}F^{(N)*}_{X/S}(\mathcal{G})
;
this
G
-bundle
\mathcal{G}
is taken to be compatible with
\omega
in a certain sense.
We will call such an enriched Cartan geometry
an
F^{N}
-Cartan geometry with model
(G,H)
(cf. Definition
3.10.2
).
Also, the notion of an
F^{\infty}
-Cartan geometry will be defined as a compatible system of
F^{N}
-Cartan geometries for various
N
.
On the other hand, we introduce
(under the assumption that
G
is affine)
dormant indigenous
(G,H)
-bundles of level
N
(cf. Defnitions
4.5.1
and
4.5.2
) in terms of both Berthelot’s PD-stratifications of higher level (cf.
[
3
]
,
[
4
]
) and the generalized notion of
p
-curvature (cf.
[
33
]
).
For each
N\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\sqcup\{\infty\}
, denote by

(10)

\displaystyle F^{N}\text{-}\mathrm{Car}_{(G,H),X/S}\ \left(\text{resp.,}\ \mathrm{Ind}^{{}^{\mathrm{Zzz...}}}_{(G,H),X/S,N}\right)

the set of isomorphism classes of
F^{N}
-Cartan geometries with model
(G,H)
(resp., dormant indigenous
(G,H)
-bundles of level
N
) on
X/S
.
Then, there exists a natural identification
F^{1}\text{-}\mathrm{Car}_{(G,H),X/S}=\mathrm{Car}_{(G,H),X/S}^{p\text{-}\mathrm{flat}}
(resp.,
\mathrm{Ind}^{{}^{\mathrm{Zzz...}}}_{(G,H),X/S,1}=\mathrm{Ind}^{{}^{\mathrm{Zzz...}}}_{(G,H),X/S}
).
In other words, the elements of
F^{N}\text{-}\mathrm{Car}_{(G,H),X/S}
(resp.,
\mathrm{Ind}^{{}^{\mathrm{Zzz...}}}_{(G,H),X/S,N}
) may be regarded as higher-level generalizations of
p
-flat Cartan geometries (resp., a dormant indigenous bundles).
By applying a generalization of Cartier’s theorem (cf. Proposition
7.7.3
),
we obtain the following theorem as an extension of Theorem
A
:

Theorem B

(cf. Propositions
3.10.5
,
3.11.2
,
4.5.4
, and Corollary
4.5.5
)
.

Suppose that
G
is affine.
Then,
for each
N\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
,
we obtain the following commutative diagram in which all the maps of sets are bijective:

Moreover, if
S=\mathrm{Spec}(k)
and
X
is proper over
k
, then the above diagram is obtained even for
N=\infty
.
Finally, the formations of these bijections commute with pull-back to étale
X
-schemes, as well as with base-change to
S
-schemes.

Before proceeding,
we shall comment on the advantage of introducing various structures of infinite level, not only those of finite level.
As observed in
[
53
]
,
the
stratified fundamental group

\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{str}}(X)
of
X
can be effectively used in the study of

F^{\infty}
-
(\mathrm{PGL}_{n+1},\mathbb{P}^{n})
-structures.
Here, the stratified fundamental group of
X
is
the Tannakian fundamental group associated with the category of
vector bundles equipped with an action of the ring of differential operators (in the sense of Grothendieck),
or equivalently, the category of
F
-divided sheaves.
Each
F^{\infty}
-
(\mathrm{PGL}_{n+1},\mathbb{P}^{n})
-structure, or the corresponding dormant indigenous
(\mathrm{PGL}_{n+1},\mathrm{PGL}_{n+1}^{\circledcirc})
-bundle of level
\infty
, specifies a
\mathrm{PGL}_{n+1}
-representation of

\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{str}}(X)
up to conjugation;
it may be thought of as the monodromy representation of this structure, and it enables us to obtain a much better understanding of
related things from the viewpoint of algebraic groups.
For example, by considering
\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{str}}(X)
, we obtained some characterizations of
\mathbb{P}^{n}
in terms of
F^{\infty}
-
(\mathrm{PGL}_{n+1},\mathbb{P}^{n})
-structures (cf.
[
53
, Theorem C, (ii)]
).
Also, it gives a complete description of the set of
F^{\infty}
-
(\mathrm{PGL}_{n+1},\mathbb{P}^{n})
-structures on an Abelian variety (cf.
[
53
, Theorem D, (ii)]
).
For a general
(G,H)
, we will prove some properties of a variety admitting an
F^{\infty}
-
(G,P)
-structure from the same viewpoint (cf. Propositions
4.6.1
and
4.6.2
).

After proving those properties, we will focus on
Frobenius-Ehresmann structures on smooth proper curves in the context of subject (ii) described in §
1.3
.
According to a result by B. Laurent (cf.
[
32
, Theorem 1.1, (1)]
), the list of homogenous curves, i.e., homogenous spaces of dimension
1
, may be described as follows (where
e
denotes the identity element of a group):

Case (a):

G=\mathrm{PGL}_{2}
,
H=\mathrm{PGL}_{2}^{\circledcirc}
;

Case (b):

G=\mathrm{Aff}_{1}
,
\mathrm{Aff}_{1}^{\circledcirc}
;

Case (c):

G=\mathbb{G}_{m}
,
H=\{e\}
;

Case (d):

G=\mathbb{G}_{a}
,
H=\{e\}
;

Case (e):

G
is an elliptic curve over
k
and
H=\{e\}
.

Here,
\mathrm{Aff}_{1}
denotes the group of affine transformations on the affine line
\mathbb{A}^{1}
and
\mathrm{Aff}_{1}^{\circledcirc}
denotes the subgroup of
\mathrm{Aff}_{1}
consisting of affine transformations fixing the origin of
\mathbb{A}^{1}
.
Cases (a) and (b) were discussed in the previous studies (cf.
[
23
]
,
[
24
]
,
[
25
]
,
[
52
]
, and
[
53
]
).
By further examining the other cases, we will classify Frobenius-Ehresmann structures on curves (cf. §
5
).
At the same time, we will formulate a conjecture (cf. Conjecture
5.1.2
) on computing the explicit number of
F^{N}
-
(\mathrm{PGL}_{2},\mathrm{PGL}_{2}^{\circledcirc})
-structures on a general smooth proper curve; this is a generalization of
Joshi’s conjecture (cf.
[
26
, Conjecture 8.1]
) for the rank
2
case, which was proved in

[
51
]
.

1.5.
Deformation theory and the Ehresmann-Weil-Thurston principle

To consider subject (iii), we will develop the deformation theory of (dormant) indigenous
(G,H)
-bundles.
The moduli stack of
dormant indigenous
(\mathrm{PGL}_{2},\mathrm{PGL}_{2}^{\circledcirc})
-bundles on genus
g
curves for each integer
g>1
(i.e.,
\mathfrak{M}_{g}^{{}^{\mathrm{Zzz...}}}\!
mentioned in Example
6.7.3
)
was substantially investigated by S. Mochizuki (cf.
[
40
]
) in the context of
p
-adic Teichmüller theory.
In that work, it was proved that this moduli stack is smooth over
k
and generically étale over the moduli stack
\mathfrak{M}_{g}
of genus
g
curves (cf.
[
40
, Chap. II, § 2.3, Theorem 2.8]
).
This fact is essential in the proof of Joshi’s conjecture described in
[
51
]
.
Indeed, we used generic étaleness to lift relevant moduli stacks to characteristic
0
and then applied a well-known formula for computing the Gromov-Witten invariant of a certain type of Quot scheme.
Also, a detailed understanding of
the moduli stack of dormant indigenous
(\mathrm{Aff}_{1},\mathrm{Aff}_{1}^{\circledcirc})
-bundles on curves was obtained by investigating
its local structure (cf.
[
52
]
).
As an application, we constructed a family of
pathological
algebraic surfaces (e.g., violating the Kodaira vanishing theorem) in characteristic
p
parametrized by a higher dimensional base space (cf.
[
52
, Theorem C]
).

In the present paper, we study the local structures of various relevant moduli spaces for a general
(G,H)
.
Part of the corresponding discussions in the holomorphic setting can be found in
[
9
]
.
Here, we will briefly examine
one of the deformation functors
that we will deal with.
(See §
6
for the other deformation functors and related results.)
Let
X
be a smooth scheme of finite type over
k
and

\mathcal{E}^{\spadesuit}:=(\mathcal{E}_{H},\nabla)
a dormant indigenous
(G,H)
-bundle on
X/k
.
Suppose that
G
is affine, and consider the functor

(15)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Def}^{{}^{\mathrm{Zzz...}}}_{(X,\mathcal{E}^{\spadesuit})}

(cf. (
354
)) classifying deformations of the pair
(X,\mathcal{E}^{\spadesuit})
preserving the
dormancy
condition.
A basic problem
regarding this functor is to determine its tangent and obstruction spaces.
These spaces can be computed
by applying an
explicit description of the hypercohomology group of a certain complex associated with
\mathcal{E}^{\spadesuit}
.
In particular, we obtain a sufficient condition for the pro-representablity of
\mathrm{Def}^{{}^{\mathrm{Zzz...}}}_{(X,\mathcal{E}^{\spadesuit})}
.
The following Theorem
C
states some results on this functor (see Propositions
6.4.3
,
6.5.2
, and
6.8.2
for the results on the other deformation functors).

Theorem C

(cf. Propositions
6.6.2
and
6.7.2
)
.

Let us keep the above notation.

(i)

Write
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{E}_{G}}
for the adjoint bundle (cf. §
7.1
) of the
G
-bundle
\mathcal{E}_{G}:=\mathcal{E}_{H}\times^{H}G
and

\nabla^{\mathrm{ad}}:\mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{E}_{G}}\rightarrow\Omega_{X/k}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{E}_{G}}
for the
k
-connection on
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{E}_{G}}
induced by
\nabla
.
Then, the tangent space of

\mathrm{Def}^{{}^{\mathrm{Zzz...}}}_{(X,\mathcal{E}^{\spadesuit})}

is isomorphic to
H^{1}(X,\mathrm{Ker}(\nabla^{\mathrm{ad}}))
, and this functor has an obstruction theory by putting

H^{2}(X,\mathrm{Ker}(\nabla^{\mathrm{ad}}))
as the obstruction space.

(ii)

Suppose that
X
is proper over
k
.
Then,
there exists a pro-representable hull for

\mathrm{Def}^{{}^{\mathrm{Zzz...}}}_{(X,\mathcal{E}^{\spadesuit})}
.

(iii)

Suppose that
G
is affine and

\mathcal{E}^{\spadesuit}
is ordinary (cf. Definition
6.7.1
).
Also, denote by
\mathrm{Def}_{X}
(cf. (
305
)) the functor classifying deformations of
X
.
Then, the morphism of functors

(16)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Def}^{{}^{\mathrm{Zzz...}}}_{(X,\mathcal{E}^{\spadesuit})}\rightarrow\mathrm{Def}_{X}

given by forgetting the data of the deformations of
\mathcal{E}^{\spadesuit}
is a semi-isomorphism (cf. Definition
6.1.4
).
In particular, if
X_{R}
is a deformation of
X
over
a local Artinian
k
-algebra
R
with residue field
k
whose maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}_{R}
is square-nilpotent,
then there exists a unique (up to isomorphism) deformation of
\mathcal{E}^{\spadesuit}
over
X_{R}
preserving the dormancy condition.

Finally, we formulate and prove the
Ehresmann-Weil-Thurston principle for Frobenius-Ehresmann structures
.
Let
X
be as above.
Notice that due to the absence of the topological fundamental group of
X
,
we have to consider
other approaches to define
G
-local systems (i.e., points of the representation variety
\mathrm{Hom}(\pi_{1}(X),G)/G
)
in an algebraic manner.
One candidate is
the category of
crystals of
G
-bundles on
the crystalline site of finite level.
Indeed, the notion of a crystal allows us to describe deformations of flat bundles
in an intrinsic way with respect to
X
, just as the notion of a topological local system on a complex manifold does via the Riemann-Hilbert correspondence.

Let us fix
a positive integer
N
and
an
F^{N}
-
(G,P)
-structure

\mathcal{S}^{\heartsuit}
on
X/k
.
Denote by

\mathcal{E}^{\diamondsuit}
the
monodromy crystal
of
\mathcal{S}^{\heartsuit}
(cf. Definition
4.5.7
).
To state the following Theorem
D
,
we shall write

(17)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Def}_{(X,\mathcal{S}^{\heartsuit})}\hskip 8.53581pt\text{and}\hskip 8.53581pt\mathrm{Def}^{{}^{\mathrm{Zzz...}}}_{\mathcal{E}^{\diamondsuit}}

(cf. (
395
), (
396
)) for the deformation functors of
(X,\mathcal{S}^{\heartsuit})
and
\mathcal{E}^{\diamondsuit}
respectively.
(The latter functor
\mathrm{Def}^{{}^{\mathrm{Zzz...}}}_{\mathcal{E}^{\diamondsuit}}\!
classifies deformations of
\mathcal{E}^{\diamondsuit}
satisfying the dormancy condition.)
Then, we will prove the following assertion, regarded as a positive characteristic version of the Ehresmann-Weil-Thurston principle; this result says that a local deformation of an
F^{N}
-
(G,P)
-structure is uniquely determined by its monodromy crystal.

Theorem D

(cf. Theorem
6.9.1
)
.

Let us keep the above notation.
Then, the morphism of deformation functors

(18)

\displaystyle\mu_{N}^{\heartsuit\Rightarrow\diamondsuit}:\mathrm{Def}_{(X,\mathcal{S}^{\heartsuit})}\rightarrow\mathrm{Def}_{\mathcal{E}^{\diamondsuit}}^{{}^{\mathrm{Zzz...}}}

given by taking monodromy crystals of deformations of
\mathcal{S}^{\heartsuit}

is a semi-isomorphism (cf. Definition
6.1.4
).
If, moreover,
\mathrm{Def}_{\mathcal{E}^{\diamondsuit}}^{{}^{\mathrm{Zzz...}}}\!
is rigid (cf. Definition
6.1.2
), then
\mu_{N}^{\heartsuit\Rightarrow\diamondsuit}
is an isomorphism.

1.6.
Organization of the present paper

In § 2, the notion of a Frobenius-Ehresmann structure is defined at the beginning (cf. Definition
2.2.2
).
We provide examples of this structure by referring to the previous work on Frobenius-Ehresmann structures modeled on
(\mathrm{PGL}_{n+1},\mathrm{PGL}_{n+1}^{\circledcirc})
and
(\mathrm{Aff}_{n},\mathrm{Aff}_{n}^{\circledcirc})
(cf. Examples
2.3.3
and
2.5.1
).
In § 3, we start by recalling the definition of the Maurer-Cantan form and then
discuss Cartan geometries in positive characteristic, including the case of higher level (cf. Definitions
3.4.1
and
3.10.2
).
A key ingredient of this study is the
p
-curvature of a vector-valued
1
-form (cf. Definition
3.7.2
).
This invariant leads us to define
p
-flat Cartan geometries (cf. Definition
3.9.1
).
The main parts of that section are the constructions of the bijections between Frobenius-Ehresmann structures and certain Cartan geometries asserted in Theorems
A
and
B
(cf.
Theorem
3.9.3
, Propositions
3.10.5
and
3.11.2
).
In §
4
, we introduce (dormant) indigenous
(G,H)
-bundles on varieties of arbitrary dimension (cf. Definitions
4.1.4
,
4.4.1
,
4.5.1
, and
4.5.2
).
The proofs of Theorems
A
and
B
are completed by constructing bijections between Cartan geometries and dormant indigenous bundles (cf.
Propositions
4.4.2
,
4.5.4
, and Corollary
4.5.5
).
After that, we verify several properties of varieties admitting an
F^{\infty}
-
(G,P)
-structure (cf. Propositions
4.6.1
and
4.6.2
).
The discussion at the end of the section deals with
F^{N}
-
(G,P)
-structures when
G
is a product of
\mathbb{G}_{m}
’s and
\mathbb{G}_{a}
’s.
As a result, it is shown (cf. Proposition
4.8.1
) that the
p
-rank and
a
-number determine whether or not such a Frobenius-Ehresmann structure exists on a given Abelian variety.
In §
5
, we consider the classification problem of Frobenius-Ehresmann structures on smooth proper curves while referring to the previous studies on
(G,H)=(\mathrm{PGL}_{2},\mathrm{PGL}_{2}^{\circledcirc}),(\mathrm{Aff}_{1},\mathrm{Aff}_{1}^{\circledcirc})
.
The content of §
6
is the deformation theory of (dormant) indigenous
(G,H)
-bundles.
First, we introduce several basic definitions in the general theory of deformation functors.
Then, we compute the tangent and obstruction spaces of various deformation functors by means of the hypercohomology groups of certain complexes (cf. Proposition
6.4.3
,
6.5.2
,
6.6.2
, and
6.8.2
).
In particular, we finish the proof of Theorem
C
.
The Ehresmann-Weil-Thurston principle for Frobenius-Ehresmann structures, i.e., Theorem
D
, is then formulated in terms of crystals and, at the same time, proved by applying the results obtained so far (cf. Theorem
6.9.1
).
In the middle of the section,
we define the ordinariness of dormant indigenous
(G,H)
-bundles (cf. Definition
6.7.1
).
However, the discussion and results therein will not be used after that.
In the Appendix, we briefly recall some of the basic definitions concerning connections on a principal bundle.
Moreover, we develop the theory of higher-level PD stratifications on a principal bundle.
This includes a generalization of Cartier’s theorem (cf. Proposition
7.7.3
).
Throughout the present paper, we will occasionally refer to definitions and facts discussed in the Appendix.

1.7.
Notation and Conventions

Throughout the present paper, we fix
an odd prime
p

and an algebraically closed field
k
of characteristic
p
.

All schemes appearing in the present paper are assumed to be locally noetherian.
Given a sheaf
\mathcal{V}
on a scheme,
we use the notation “
v\in\mathcal{V}
”
for a local section
v
of
\mathcal{V}
.
If
S
is a scheme and
X
is a scheme over
S
, then we denote by
\Omega_{X/S}
(resp.,
\mathcal{T}_{X/S}
) the sheaf of
1
-forms (resp., the sheaf of vector fields) on
X
relative to
S
.
In §§
2
-
6
,
we will always be working over
k
unless stated otherwise; for example, products of
k
-schemes will be taken over
k
, i.e.,
X_{1}\times X_{2}:=X_{1}\times_{k}X_{2}
.
Given a
k
-scheme
X
, we write
\Omega_{X}:=\Omega_{X/k}
and
\mathcal{T}_{X}:=\mathcal{T}_{X/k}
for simplicity.
Also, for a
k
-vector space
M
and an
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-module
\mathcal{F}
, we write
\mathcal{F}\otimes M:=\mathcal{F}\otimes_{k}M
.

Let
S
be a
k
-scheme,
X
a smooth
S
-scheme,
M
a
k
-vector space, and
q
a nonnegative integer.
Then,
an
M
-valued
q
-form on
X/S

is defined as a global section of the sheaf
(\bigwedge^{q}\Omega_{X/S})\otimes M
, where
\bigwedge^{q}\Omega_{X/S}
denotes the
q
-th exterior power of
\Omega_{X/S}
over
\mathcal{O}_{X}
.
For an
M
-valued
q
-form
\omega
on
X/S
, we shall write

\omega^{\triangleright}
for the
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-linear morphism
\bigwedge^{q}\mathcal{T}_{X/S}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{X}\otimes M
corresponding to
\omega
.

Given an algebraic group, we will often use the notation
e
to denote its identity element.
In §§
2
-
6
, we fix
a connected smooth algebraic group
G
over
k
and a closed subgroup
H
of
G
such that
the homogenous space
P
defined as the quotient
G/H
is smooth over
k
.
We will write

\mathfrak{g}\left(:=\mathrm{Lie}(G)\right)
for the Lie algebra of
G
, i.e., the tangent space
\mathcal{T}_{G}|_{e}\left(=e^{*}(\mathcal{T}_{G})\right)
at the identity element
e
and also write
\mathfrak{h}:=\mathrm{Lie}(H)
, which defines a Lie subalgebra of
\mathfrak{g}
.
Also, we write

\pi_{G}
for the natural projection
G\twoheadrightarrow P
.

Denote by
\mathbb{G}_{m}
the multiplicative group over
k
and by
\mathbb{G}_{a}
the additive group over
k
.
Also, denote by
\mu_{p}
the subgroup of
\mathbb{G}_{m}
consisting of
p
-th roots of unity and
by
\alpha_{p}
the subgroup of
\mathbb{G}_{a}
consisting of elements
a
with
a^{p}=0
.

Let
X
be a scheme and
G^{\prime}
an algebraic group.
By a
G^{\prime}
-bundle
on
X
, we mean
a principal
G^{\prime}
-bundle (equipped with a right
G^{\prime}
-action) on
X
in the étale topology.
Let
g:G^{\prime}\rightarrow G^{\prime\prime}
be a morphism of algebraic groups.
Then, given a principal
G^{\prime}
-bundle
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
on
X
, we shall write
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\times^{G^{\prime}}G^{\prime\prime}
for the
G^{\prime\prime}
-bundle on
X
obtained from
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
via change of structure group by
g
.
Also, for a
G^{\prime\prime}
-bundle
\mathcal{E}^{\prime\prime}
on
X
, a
G^{\prime}
-reduction
of
\mathcal{E}^{\prime\prime}

is defined as a
G^{\prime}
-bundle
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
on
X

together with an isomorphism
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\times^{G^{\prime}}G^{\prime\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathcal{E}^{\prime\prime}
of
G^{\prime\prime}
-bundles.

Let
X
and
G^{\prime}
be as above and let

h:X\rightarrow G^{\prime}
be a morphism of schemes.
Then, we write

\mathrm{R}_{h}
(resp.,
\mathrm{L}_{h}
)
for the right-translation (resp., the left-translation) by
h
, i.e., the automorphism of
X\times G
given by
(x,a)\mapsto(x,a\cdot h)
(resp.,
(x,a)\mapsto(x,h\cdot a)
) for any
x\in X
and
a\in G
.
If
\mathcal{E}
is a
G
-bundle on
X
and
h
is a point of
G
, then
we use the notation
\mathrm{R}_{h}
to denote the automorphism of
\mathcal{E}
induced from the action by
h
.

2.
Frobenius-Ehresmann structures

In this section, we introduce Frobenius-Ehresmann structures of various levels, which generalize the Frobenius-projective and Frobenius-affine structures discussed in
[
23
]
,
[
24
]
, and
[
53
]
.
Let us fix a
k
-scheme
S
and a smooth scheme
X
over
S
.

2.1.
Frobenius twists and relative Frobenius morphisms

We shall write
f:X\rightarrow S
for the structure morphism of
X/S
and

F_{X}
(resp.,
F_{S}
) for
the absolute Frobenius endomorphism of
X
(resp.,
S
).
For each positive integer
N
, the
N
-th Frobenius twist of
X
over
S
is, by definition, the base-change
X^{(N)}\left(:=X\times_{S,F_{S}^{N}}S\right)
of
X
via
the
N
-th iterate
F_{S}^{N}
of
F_{S}
.
Denote by
f^{(N)}:X^{(N)}\rightarrow S
the structure morphism of

X^{(N)}/S
, i.e.,
f^{(N)}:=f\times\mathrm{id}_{S}
.
The
N
-th relative Frobenius morphism of
X
over
S
is the unique morphism
F_{X/S}^{(N)}:X\rightarrow X^{(N)}
over
S
that makes the following diagram commute:

For simplicity, we shall write
F_{X/S}:=F^{(1)}_{X/S}
.
Also, let
(X^{(0)},F_{X}^{(0)}):=(X,\mathrm{id}_{X})
for convenience.

Denote by

G_{X}
the group-valued étale sheaf on
X
represented by
G
, i.e.,
G_{X}:=\mathrm{Hom}_{k}(-,G)
.
Moreover, given a positive integer
N
, we shall write

(25)

\displaystyle G_{X/S}^{(N)}:=(F^{(N)}_{X/S})^{-1}(G_{X^{(N)}})\left(\subseteq G_{X}\right).

The sheaves
G_{X/S}^{(N)}
for various
N
constitute, via pull-back by relative Frobenius morphisms,
a sequence of inclusions

(26)

\displaystyle G_{X}\supseteq G_{X/S}^{(1)}\supseteq G_{X/S}^{(2)}\supseteq\cdots\supseteq G_{X/S}^{(N)}\supseteq\cdots.

2.2.
F^{N}
-
(G,P)
-structures for
N<\infty

We shall
write

(27)

\displaystyle P_{S}:=S\times P,

and write
\mathrm{L}_{P_{S}}
for the left
G
-action

G\times P_{S}\rightarrow P_{S}
on the
S
-scheme
P_{S}
induced by the natural left
G
-action on
P
.
We shall set

(28)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{\text{\'{e}t}}_{P,X/S}

to be the set-valued étale sheaf on
X
that assigns, to
each étale scheme
U
over
X
, the set
of étale
S
-morphisms from
U
to
P_{S}
.
When there is no fear of confusion, we will denote this sheaf by
\mathcal{P}^{\text{\'{e}t}}
for simplicity.
Now, let us fix a positive integer
N
.

Lemma 2.2.1
.

Let
U
be an étale scheme over
X
,

\phi

an
S
-morphism
U\rightarrow P_{S}
,
and
h
an element of
G_{X/S}^{(N)}(U)\left(=G(U^{(N)})\right)
.
Write
h^{F}:=h\circ F_{U/S}^{(N)}:U\rightarrow G
and define
h(\phi)
to be
the composite

(29)

\displaystyle h(\phi):U\xrightarrow{(h^{F},\phi)}G\times P_{S}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{L}_{P_{S}}}P_{S}.

Then,
\phi\in\mathcal{P}^{\text{\'{e}t}}(U)
implies

h(\phi)\in\mathcal{P}^{\text{\'{e}t}}(U)
.

Proof.

Suppose that
\phi\in\mathcal{P}^{\text{\'{e}t}}(U)
, and
let us choose a point
q\in U(k)
.
The differential of
(h^{F},\phi):U\rightarrow G\times P_{S}
at
q
defines a morphism of
k
-vector spaces

(30)

\displaystyle(dh^{F},d\phi):\mathcal{T}_{U/S}|_{q}\rightarrow\mathcal{T}_{G/k}|_{h^{F}(q)}\oplus\mathcal{T}_{P_{S}/S}|_{\phi(q)}\left(=\mathcal{T}_{(G\times P_{S})/S}|_{(h^{F}(q),\phi(q))}\right).

Since
h^{F}
factors through
F_{U/S}^{(N)}:U\rightarrow U^{(N)}
,
the first component
dh^{F}:\mathcal{T}_{U/S}|_{q}\rightarrow\mathcal{T}_{G/k}|_{h^{F}(q)}
of this morphism becomes the zero map.
On the other hand, the étaleness assumption on
\phi
implies the bijectivity of
the second component
d\phi:\mathcal{T}_{U/S}|_{q}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathcal{T}_{P_{S}/S}|_{\phi(q)}
.
Also, the differential
d\mathrm{L}_{P_{S}}:\mathcal{T}_{G/k}|_{h^{F}(q)}\oplus\mathcal{T}_{P_{S}/S}|_{\phi(q)}\rightarrow\mathcal{T}_{P_{S}/S}|_{h(\phi)(q)}
of
\mathrm{L}_{P_{S}}

is restricted to the identity morphism
\mathcal{T}_{P_{S}/S}|_{\phi(q)}\left(=0\oplus\mathcal{T}_{P_{S}/S}|_{\phi(q)}\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathcal{T}_{P_{S}/S}|_{h(\phi)(q)}
of
\mathcal{T}_{P_{S}/S}|_{\phi(q)}
.
It follows that the differential
d(h(\phi))\left(=d\mathrm{L}_{P_{S}}\circ(dh^{F},d\phi)\right)
of
h(\phi)
at
q
is bijective.
Hence, since both
U
and
P_{S}
are smooth over
S
, the morphism
h(\phi)
turns out to be étale.
This completes the proof of this lemma.
∎

By the above lemma, the assignment
(h,\phi)\mapsto h(\phi)
defines a left
G_{X/S}^{(N)}
-action

(31)

\displaystyle\mathrm{L}_{\mathcal{P}}^{(N)}:G_{X/S}^{(N)}\times\mathcal{P}^{\text{\'{e}t}}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}^{\text{\'{e}t}}

on
\mathcal{P}^{\text{\'{e}t}}
.
The formation of this action for each
N
commutes with the inclusions in (
26
).

Definition 2.2.2
.

Let
\mathcal{S}^{\heartsuit}
be a subsheaf of
\mathcal{P}^{\text{\'{e}t}}
.
We shall say that

\mathcal{S}^{\heartsuit}
is
a
Frobenius-Ehresmann
(G,P)
-structure of level
N
, or simply
an
F^{N}
-
(G,P)
-structure
,
on
X/S
if
\mathcal{S}^{\heartsuit}
is closed under the
G_{X/S}^{(N)}
-action
\mathrm{L}_{\mathcal{P}}^{(N)}

and forms a left
G_{X/S}^{(N)}
-torsor on
X
with respect to the resulting
G_{X/S}^{(N)}
-action on
\mathcal{S}^{\heartsuit}
.
In the case of
N=1
, it is called an
F
-
(G,P)
-structure.

We shall denote by

(32)

\displaystyle F^{N}\text{-}\mathrm{Ehr}_{(G,P),X/S}

the set of
F^{N}
-
(G,P)
-structures
on
X/S
.
To simplify the notation slightly, we will write

F\text{-}\mathrm{Ehr}_{(G,P),X/S}

:=F^{1}\text{-}\mathrm{Ehr}_{(G,P),X/S}
.
Note that
X/S
admits an
F^{N}
-
(G,P)
-structure only when the relative dimension of
X/S
coincides with the dimension of
G
.

2.3.
F^{N}
-
(G,P)
-structures for
N=\infty

Let
N
and
N^{\prime}
be positive integers with
N\leq N^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{S}^{\heartsuit}
an
F^{N^{\prime}}
-
(G,P)
-structure on
X/S
.
Denote by

(33)

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{\heartsuit}|^{\langle N\rangle}

the smallest subsheaf of
\mathcal{P}^{\text{\'{e}t}}
which contains
\mathcal{S}^{\heartsuit}
and is closed under the
G_{X/S}^{(N)}
-action.
This sheaf can be verified to form an
F^{N}
-
(G,P)
-structure on
X/S
.
We refer to
\mathcal{S}^{\heartsuit}|^{\langle N\rangle}
as the
N
-th truncation
of
\mathcal{S}^{\heartsuit}
.
The resulting assignments
\mathcal{S}^{\heartsuit}\mapsto\mathcal{S}^{\heartsuit}|^{\langle N\rangle}
for various pairs
(N,N^{\prime})

give a projective system of sets

(34)

\displaystyle\cdots\rightarrow F^{N}\text{-}\mathrm{Ehr}_{(G,P),X/S}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow F^{2}\text{-}\mathrm{Ehr}_{(G,P),X/S}\rightarrow F^{1}\text{-}\mathrm{Ehr}_{(G,P),X/S}.

Definition 2.3.1
.

A
Frobenius-Ehresmann
(G,P)
-structure of level
\infty
, or simply
an
F^{\infty}
-
(G,P)
-structure
, on
X/S
is a collection

(35)

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{\heartsuit}_{\infty}:=\{\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\heartsuit}\}_{N\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}},

where each
\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\heartsuit}
(
N\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
) is an
F^{N}
-
(G,P)
-structure on
X/S
with
\mathcal{S}^{\heartsuit}_{N+1}|^{\langle N\rangle}=\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\heartsuit}
.

Denote by

(36)

\displaystyle F^{\infty}\text{-}\mathrm{Ehr}_{(G,P),X/S}

the set of
F^{\infty}
-
(G,P)
-structures on
X/S
; this set
may be naturally identified with the limit of the projective system (
34
), i.e.,

(37)

\displaystyle F^{\infty}\text{-}\mathrm{Ehr}_{(G,P),X/S}=\varprojlim_{N\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}}F^{N}\text{-}\mathrm{Ehr}_{(G,P),X/S}.

Example 2.3.2

(Standard example)
.

For each positive integer
N
,
we shall construct a standard example of an
F^{N}
-
(G,P)
-structure defined on the homogenous space
P
.
Let us set

(38)

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{triv},\heartsuit}_{N}

to be the subsheaf of
\mathcal{P}_{P,P_{S}/S}^{\text{\'{e}t}}
which, to any étale scheme
U
over
P_{S}
, assigns the set

(39)

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{triv},\heartsuit}_{N}(U):=\left\{h(\phi_{U})\in\mathcal{P}_{P,P_{S}/S}^{\text{\'{e}t}}(U)\,\Big|\,h\in G_{X/S}^{(N)}(U)\right\},

where
\phi_{U}
denotes the structure morphism
U\rightarrow P_{S}
of the
P_{S}
-scheme
U
.
That is to say,
\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{triv},\heartsuit}_{N}
is the smallest subsheaf of
\mathcal{P}_{P,P_{S}/S}^{\text{\'{e}t}}
that is closed under the
G_{P_{S}/S}^{(N)}
-action and contains the global section determined by the identity morphism of
P_{S}
.
Then,
\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{triv},\heartsuit}_{N}
specifies an
F^{N}
-
(G,P)
-structure on
P_{S}/S
.
The formation of
\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{triv},\heartsuit}_{N}
commutes with truncation to lower levels, so the collection

(40)

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{triv},\heartsuit}_{\infty}:=\{\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{triv},\heartsuit}_{N}\}_{N\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}}

forms an
F^{\infty}
-
(G,P)
-structure on
P_{S}/S
.

Example 2.3.3
.

Recall the Frobenius-Ehresmann structures for the special cases discussed in
[
53
]
.
Let
n
be a positive integer and let
N\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\sqcup\{\infty\}
.

(i)

Denote by
\mathbb{P}^{n}
the
n
-dimensional projective space over
k
.
In particular, the
k
-rational points of
\mathbb{P}^{n}
correspond bijectively to the ratios
[a_{0}:a_{1}:\cdots:a_{n}]
with
a_{0},\cdots,a_{n}\in k
and
(a_{0},\cdots,a_{n})\neq(0,\cdots,0)
.
The rank
n+1
projective linear group

\mathrm{PGL}_{n+1}
over
k

may be identified with the automorphism group of
\mathbb{P}^{n}
in such a way that

\overline{A}[a_{0}:\cdots:a_{n}]:=[a_{0}:\cdots:a_{n}]\overline{{{}^{t}}A}
for each
A\in\mathrm{GL}_{n+1}
, where
\overline{(-)}
denotes the natural projection
\mathrm{GL}_{n+1}\twoheadrightarrow\mathrm{PGL}_{n+1}
.
Denote by

(41)

\displaystyle\mathrm{PGL}_{n+1}^{\circledcirc}

the subgroup of
\mathrm{PGL}_{n+1}
consisting of automorphisms of
\mathbb{P}^{n}

fixing
[1:0:0:\cdots:0]
.
The homogenous space
\mathrm{PGL}_{n+1}/\mathrm{PGL}_{n+1}^{\circledcirc}
is naturally isomorphic to
\mathbb{P}^{n}
.
Thus, we obtain the notion of an
F^{N}
-
(\mathrm{PGL}_{n+1},\mathbb{P}^{n})
-structure, which is equivalent to what we call an
F^{N}
-projective structure (cf.
[
53
, § 1, Definition 1.2.1]
) under the assumption that
X
is a smooth quasi-projective
k
-variety of dimension
n
with
p\nmid(n+1)
.
These notions differ in that, on the one hand
an
F^{N}
-
(\mathrm{PGL}_{n+1},\mathbb{P}^{n})
-structure is an
étale
sheaf of locally defined étale morphisms to
\mathbb{P}^{n}
,
but on the other hand an
F^{N}
-projective structure is defined in
loc. cit.
as a
Zariski
sheaf.
However, the result of
[
53
, Theorem A (and § 3, Corollary 3.4.4)]
together with Theorem
B
in the present paper shows that these definitions turn out to be equivalent.
That is to say, under the assumption described above, the underlying
\mathrm{PGL}_{n+1}
-torsor of any
F^{N}
-
(\mathrm{PGL}_{n+1},\mathbb{P}^{n})
-structure may be trivialized in the Zariski topology.

(ii)

Next,
denote by
\mathbb{A}^{n}
the
n
-dimensional affine space over
k
.
Also, denote by
\mathrm{Aff}_{n}
the group of affine transformations on
\mathbb{A}^{n}
;
it can be described concretely as the semidirect product of
\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\times n}
(:= the group of translations on
\mathbb{A}^{n}
) by
\mathrm{GL}_{n}
(:= the rank
n
general linear group over
k
):

(42)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Aff}_{n}=\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\times n}\rtimes\mathrm{GL}_{n},

where the
\mathrm{GL}_{n}
-action on
\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\times n}\left(=\mathbb{A}^{n}\right)
is taken to mean matrix multiplication of a vector.
If

(43)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Aff}_{n}^{\circledcirc}

denotes the subgroup of
\mathrm{Aff}_{n}
consisting of affine transformations fixing the origin, then the homogenous space
\mathrm{Aff}_{n}/\mathrm{Aff}_{n}^{\circledcirc}
is naturally isomorphic to
\mathbb{A}^{n}
.
Thus, we obtain
the notion of an
F^{N}
-
(\mathrm{Aff}_{n},\mathbb{A}^{n})
-structure.
Just as in the above case, this is equivalent to what we call an
F^{N}
-affine structure
(cf.
[
51
, § 1, Definition 1.2.1, and § 3, Corollary 3.4.4]
).

In
[
53
]
, we showed various fundamental properties of a variety admitting either of these structures by establishing bijective correspondences with other equivalent realizations (cf.
[
53
, Theorem A]
).
For example, we proved formulas providing necessary conditions on (crystalline) Chern classes for the existence of an
F^{N}
-projective or
F^{N}
-affine structure (cf.
[
53
, Theorem B]
).
Moreover, we investigated the classification problems of curves, surfaces, and Abelian varieties admitting
such a structure
(cf.
[
53
, Theorems E, F]
).

2.4.
Pull-back and base-change of
F^{N}
-
(G,P)
-structures

Consider the pull-back of a given Frobenius-Ehresmann
(G,P)
-structure by an étale morphism between the underlying schemes.
Let
Y
be a smooth scheme over
S
and
y:Y\rightarrow X
an étale
S
-morphism.
Given each étale
S
-morphism
\phi:U\rightarrow P_{S}
from an étale
X
-scheme
U
to
P_{S}
,
we obtain the étale composite
\phi\circ y:y^{-1}(U)\left(:=Y\times_{X}U\right)\rightarrow P_{S}
;
the assignment
\phi\mapsto\phi\circ y
yields a morphism

(44)

\displaystyle y^{-1}(\mathcal{P}_{P,X/S}^{\text{\'{e}t}})\rightarrow\mathcal{P}_{P,Y/S}^{\text{\'{e}t}}

of étale sheaves on
Y
.
For an
F^{N}
-
(G,P)
-structure
\mathcal{S}^{\heartsuit}
on
X/S
with
N\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
,
we set

(45)

\displaystyle y^{*}(\mathcal{S}^{\heartsuit})

to be the subsheaf of
\mathcal{P}_{P,Y/S}^{\text{\'{e}t}}
defined to be the smallest subsheaf that is closed under the
G_{Y/S}^{(N)}
-action and contains the image of
y^{-1}(\mathcal{S}^{\heartsuit})
via (
44
).
One may verify that
y^{*}(\mathcal{S}^{\heartsuit})
specifies an
F^{N}
-
(G,P)
-structure on
Y/S
,
and the formation of
y^{*}(\mathcal{S}^{\heartsuit})
commutes with truncation to lower levels.
This implies that, for each
F^{\infty}
-
(G,P)
-structure
\mathcal{S}^{\heartsuit}:=\{\mathcal{S}^{\heartsuit}_{N}\}_{N\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}}
,
the collection
y^{*}(\mathcal{S}^{\heartsuit}):=\{y^{*}(\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\heartsuit})\}_{N}
forms an
F^{\infty}
-
(G,P)
-structure on
Y/S
.
In each case of
N\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\sqcup\{\infty\}
,
y^{*}(\mathcal{S}^{\heartsuit})
is called the
pull-back
of
\mathcal{S}^{\heartsuit}
via
y
, and the assignment
\mathcal{S}^{\heartsuit}\mapsto y^{*}(\mathcal{S}^{\heartsuit})
defines a map of sets

(46)

\displaystyle F^{N}\text{-}\mathrm{Ehr}_{(G,P),X/S}\rightarrow F^{N}\text{-}\mathrm{Ehr}_{(G,P),Y/S}.

Next, consider the base-change of a Frobenius-Ehresmann
(G,P)
-structure.
Let
s:S^{\prime}\rightarrow S
be a morphism of
k
-schemes.
Given each étale
S
-morphism
\phi:U\rightarrow P_{S}
from an étale
X
-scheme
U
to
P_{S}
, we obtain the base-change
s^{*}(\phi):S^{\prime}\times_{S}U\rightarrow P_{S^{\prime}}\left(=S^{\prime}\times_{S}P_{S}\right)
, which is étale;
the assignment
\phi\mapsto s^{*}(\phi)
yields a morphism

(47)

\displaystyle s_{X}^{-1}(\mathcal{P}_{P,X/S}^{\text{\'{e}t}})\rightarrow\mathcal{P}_{P,X^{\prime}/S^{\prime}}^{\text{\'{e}t}}

of étale sheaves on
X^{\prime}:=S^{\prime}\times_{S}X
, where
s_{X}
denotes the natural projection
X^{\prime}\rightarrow X
.
For an
F^{N}
-
(G,P)
-structure
\mathcal{S}^{\heartsuit}
on
X/S
with
N\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
,
we shall set

(48)

\displaystyle s^{*}(\mathcal{S}^{\heartsuit})

to be
the smallest subsheaf of
\mathcal{P}^{\text{\'{e}t}}_{P,X^{\prime}/S^{\prime}}
that is closed under the
G_{X^{\prime}/S^{\prime}}^{(N)}
-action and contains the image of
s_{X}^{-1}(\mathcal{S}^{\heartsuit})
via (
47
).
One may verify that
s^{*}(\mathcal{S}^{\heartsuit})
specifies an
F^{N}
-
(G,P)
-structure on
X^{\prime}/S^{\prime}
, and
the formation of
s^{*}(\mathcal{S}^{\heartsuit})
commutes with truncation to lower levels.
This implies that, for each
F^{\infty}
-
(G,P)
-structure
\mathcal{S}^{\heartsuit}:=\{\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\heartsuit}\}_{N\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}}
, the collection
s^{*}(\mathcal{S}^{\heartsuit}):=\{s^{*}(\mathcal{S}^{\heartsuit}_{N})\}_{N}
forms an
F^{\infty}
-
(G,P)
-structure on
X^{\prime}/S^{\prime}
.
In each case of
N\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\sqcup\{\infty\}
,

s^{*}(\mathcal{S}^{\heartsuit})
is called the
base-change
of
\mathcal{S}^{\heartsuit}
, and the assignment
\mathcal{S}^{\heartsuit}\mapsto s^{*}(\mathcal{S}^{\heartsuit})
defines a map of sets

(49)

\displaystyle F^{N}\text{-}\mathrm{Ehr}_{(G,P),X/S}\rightarrow F^{N}\text{-}\mathrm{Ehr}_{(G,P),X^{\prime}/S^{\prime}}.

2.5.
Change of structure group

Let
G^{\prime}
be another connected smooth algebraic group over
k
and
H^{\prime}
a closed subgroup of
G^{\prime}
such that the quotient
P^{\prime}:=G^{\prime}/H^{\prime}
is smooth over
k
.
Moreover, suppose that we are given a morphism of
k
-groups
g:G\rightarrow G^{\prime}
such that
g(H)\subseteq H^{\prime}
and the
k
-morphism
\overline{g}:P\rightarrow P^{\prime}
induced by
g
is étale.

Now, let
\mathcal{S}^{\heartsuit}
be an
F^{N}
-
(G,P)
-structure on
X/S
with
N\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
.
Denote by

(50)

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{\heartsuit}\times^{P}P^{\prime}

the smallest subsheaf of
\mathcal{P}_{P^{\prime},X/S}^{\text{\'{e}t}}
that is closed under the
G^{\prime(N)}_{X/S}
-action on
\mathcal{P}_{P^{\prime},X/S}^{\text{\'{e}t}}
and contains the sections
\overline{g}\circ\phi
for various local sections
\phi\in\mathcal{S}^{\heartsuit}
.
One may verify that
\mathcal{S}^{\heartsuit}\times^{P}P^{\prime}
defines an
F^{N}
-
(G^{\prime},H^{\prime})
-structure on
X/S
, and
the formation of
\mathcal{S}^{\heartsuit}\times^{P}P^{\prime}
commutes with truncation to lower levels.
This implies that, for each
F^{\infty}
-
(G,P)
-structure
\mathcal{S}^{\heartsuit}:=\{\mathcal{S}^{\heartsuit}_{N}\}_{N}
on
X/S
,
the collection
\mathcal{S}^{\heartsuit}\times^{P}P^{\prime}:=\{\mathcal{S}^{\heartsuit}_{N}\times^{P}P^{\prime}\}_{N}
forms
an
F^{\infty}
-
(G^{\prime},P^{\prime})
-structure on
X/S
.
In each case of
N\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\sqcup\{\infty\}
,
the assignment
\mathcal{S}^{\heartsuit}\mapsto\mathcal{S}^{\heartsuit}\times^{P}P^{\prime}
defines a map of sets

(51)

\displaystyle F^{N}\text{-}\mathrm{Ehr}_{(G,P),X/S}\rightarrow F^{N}\text{-}\mathrm{Ehr}_{(G^{\prime},P^{\prime}),X/S}.

Example 2.5.1
.

We shall identify
\mathbb{A}^{n}
with the open subscheme of
\mathbb{P}^{n}
consisting of the elements
\{[1:a_{1}:\cdots:a_{n}]\,|\,a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}\in k\}
.
Consider the morphism of
k
-groups
\mathrm{Aff}_{n}\rightarrow\mathrm{PGL}_{n+1}
given by
({\bf a},A)\mapsto\overline{\begin{pmatrix}1&{\bf 0}\\
{{}^{t}}{\bf a}&A\end{pmatrix}}
for each
{\bf a}\in\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\times n}
and
A\in\mathrm{GL}_{n}
.
By this morphism,

\mathrm{Aff}_{n}
can be regarded as the subgroup of
\mathrm{PGL}_{n+1}
consisting of automorphisms
h:\mathbb{P}^{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathbb{P}^{n}
with
h(\mathbb{A}^{n})=\mathbb{A}^{n}
.
The inclusion
\mathrm{Aff}_{n}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{PGL}_{n+1}
induces a morphism
\mathrm{Aff}_{n}/\mathrm{Aff}_{n}^{\circledcirc}\rightarrow\mathrm{PGL}_{n+1}/\mathrm{PGL}_{n+1}^{\circledcirc}
, which becomes the open immersion
\mathbb{A}^{n}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{n}
under the natural identifications
\mathrm{Aff}_{n}/\mathrm{Aff}_{n}^{\circledcirc}=\mathbb{A}^{n}
,
\mathrm{PGL}_{n+1}/\mathrm{PGL}_{n+1}^{\circledcirc}=\mathbb{P}^{n}
.
In particular, for each
N\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\sqcup\{\infty\}
, we obtain a map of sets

(52)

\displaystyle F^{N}\text{-}\mathrm{Ehr}_{(\mathrm{Aff}_{n},\mathbb{A}^{n}),X/S}\rightarrow F^{N}\text{-}\mathrm{Ehr}_{(\mathrm{PGL}_{n+1},\mathbb{P}^{n}),X/S}.

This map is, in general, neither surjective nor injective.
In fact, we know that it is not surjective (resp., not injective) when
N=\infty
and
X/k
is the projective space
\mathbb{P}^{n}
(resp., an ordinary Abelian variety) (cf.
[
53
, Theorems C and D]
).

Example 2.5.2
.

Next, let us consider the morphism of algebraic
k
-groups

(53)

\displaystyle\rho:\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\times n}\rightarrow\mathrm{Aff}_{n};(u_{1},u_{2},\cdots,u_{n})\mapsto\overline{\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0&\cdots&0\\
1-u_{1}&u_{1}&0&\cdots&0\\
1-u_{2}&0&u_{2}&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
1-u_{n}&0&0&\cdots&u_{n}\end{pmatrix}}.

Under the natural identification
\mathrm{Aff}_{n}/\mathrm{Aff}_{n}^{\circledcirc}=\mathbb{A}^{n}
,
the
k
-morphism
\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\times n}\left(=\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\times n}/\{e\}\right)\rightarrow\mathrm{Aff}_{n}/\mathrm{Aff}_{n}^{\circledcirc}
induced by
\rho
coincides with
the open immersion
\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\times n}\hookrightarrow\left((\mathbb{A}^{1})^{\times n}=\right)\mathbb{A}^{n}
given by
(u_{1},u_{2},\cdots,u_{n})

\mapsto(1-u_{1},1-u_{2},\cdots,1-u_{n})
.
In particular, for each
N\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\sqcup\{\infty\}
, we obtain the map of sets

(54)

\displaystyle F^{N}\text{-}\mathrm{Ehr}_{(\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\times n},\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\times n}),X/S}\rightarrow F^{N}\text{-}\mathrm{Ehr}_{(\mathrm{Aff}_{n},\mathbb{A}^{n}),X/S}.

Now, denote by
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
the symmetric group of
n
letters.
Each element
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
determines an automorphism
\sigma^{*}
of
\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\times n}
given by permuting the components according to
\sigma
; it induces a bijective endomap of
F^{N}\text{-}\mathrm{Ehr}_{(\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\times n},\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\times n}),X/S}
.
In this way, we obtain an action of
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
on this set, and hence obtain the quotient set
F^{N}\text{-}\mathrm{Ehr}_{(\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\times n},\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\times n}),X/S}/\mathfrak{S}_{n}
.
It is verified that (
54
) factors through the natural surjection

F^{N}\text{-}\mathrm{Ehr}_{(\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\times n},\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\times n}),X/S}\twoheadrightarrow F^{N}\text{-}\mathrm{Ehr}_{(\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\times n},\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\times n}),X/S}/\mathfrak{S}_{n}
.
Thus, (
54
) yields a map

(55)

\displaystyle F^{N}\text{-}\mathrm{Ehr}_{(\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\times n},\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\times n}),X/S}/\mathfrak{S}_{n}\rightarrow F^{N}\text{-}\mathrm{Ehr}_{(\mathrm{Aff}_{n},\mathbb{A}^{n}),X/S}.

According to
[
53
, Theorem D, (ii)]
, this map is bijective when
N=\infty
and
X
is an ordinary Abelian variety over
\mathrm{Spec}(k)=S
(cf. Proposition
4.8.2
, (i)).

3.
Cartan geometries in positive characteristic

In this section, we study Cartan geometries in positive characteristic, including the case of higher level.
The main results of this section provide bijective correspondences between Frobenius-Ehresmann structures and certain Cartan geometries (cf. Theorem
3.9.3
and Proposition
3.10.5
).
Let
S
be a
k
-scheme and
f:X\rightarrow S
a smooth scheme over
S
.

3.1.
The Maurer-Cartan form

In what follows, we occasionally identify the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
with the space
\Gamma(G,\mathcal{T}_{G})^{G}
of vector fields on
G
invariant under the left-translations
\mathrm{L}_{(-)}
.
That is to say, given any element
v\in\mathfrak{g}=\mathcal{T}_{G}|_{e}
, we can use left-translation to produce a global vector field
v^{\dagger}
on
G
with
v^{\dagger}|_{h}=d\mathrm{L}_{h}|_{e}(v)
for every
h\in G
, where
d\mathrm{L}_{h}|_{e}
denotes the
k
-linear morphism
\mathcal{T}_{G}|_{e}\left(=e^{*}(\mathcal{T}_{G})\right)\rightarrow\mathcal{T}_{G}|_{h}\left(=h^{*}(\mathcal{T}_{G})\right)
defined as the differential of
\mathrm{L}_{h}
at
e
.
Then, there exists
an
\mathcal{O}_{G}
-linear isomorphism

(56)

\displaystyle\omega_{G}^{\triangleright}:\mathcal{T}_{G}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathcal{O}_{G}\otimes\mathfrak{g}

determined uniquely
by
\omega_{G}^{\triangleright}(v^{\dagger})=1\otimes v
for any
v\in\mathfrak{g}
.
This morphism corresponds to a
\mathfrak{g}
-valued
1
-form

(57)

\displaystyle\omega_{G}\in\Gamma(G,\Omega_{G}\otimes\mathfrak{g})\left(\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{O}_{G}}(\mathcal{T}_{G},\mathcal{O}_{G}\otimes\mathfrak{g})\right),

which is called the
(left-invariant) Maurer-Cartan form
on
G
(cf.
[
47
, Chap. 3, § 1, Definition 1.3]
).
The assertions in the following proposition are well-known in the differential (and holomorphic) geometric setting.

Proposition 3.1.1
.

(i)

For any
h\in G(k)
,
the following square diagrams are commutative:

\textstyle{\mathcal{T}_{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\scriptstyle{\omega^{\triangleright}_{G}}
\scriptstyle{\wr}
\scriptstyle{d\mathrm{L}_{h}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{O}_{G}\otimes\mathfrak{g}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\wr}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{L}_{h}^{*}(\mathcal{T}_{G})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{L}_{h}^{*}(\omega_{G}^{\triangleright})}
\textstyle{\mathcal{O}_{G}\otimes\mathfrak{g},}

\textstyle{\mathcal{T}_{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\scriptstyle{\omega_{G}^{\triangleright}}
\scriptstyle{\wr}
\scriptstyle{d\mathrm{R}_{h}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{O}_{G}\otimes\mathfrak{g}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\wr}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}\otimes\mathrm{Ad}_{G}(h^{-1})}
\textstyle{\mathrm{R}_{h}^{*}(\mathcal{T}_{G})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{R}_{h}^{*}(\omega^{\triangleright}_{G})}
\textstyle{\mathcal{O}_{G}\otimes\mathfrak{g}}

(cf. (
404
) for the definition of
\mathrm{Ad}_{G}
).

(ii)

Let
G^{\prime}
be another connected smooth algebraic group over
k
with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
and
g:G\rightarrow G^{\prime}
a morphism of
k
-groups.
Then, the Maurer-Cartan form
\omega_{G^{\prime}}
on
G^{\prime}
makes the following square diagram commute:

\textstyle{\mathcal{T}_{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\scriptstyle{\omega_{G}^{\triangleright}}
\scriptstyle{dg}
\textstyle{\mathcal{O}_{G}\otimes\mathfrak{g}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}\otimes dg|_{e}}
\textstyle{g^{*}(\mathcal{T}_{G^{\prime}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\scriptstyle{g^{*}(\omega_{G^{\prime}}^{\triangleright})}
\textstyle{\mathcal{O}_{G}\otimes\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}.}

Proof.

Let
v
and
a
be arbitrary elements of
\mathfrak{g}
and
G(k)
respectively.
Observe that

(70)

\displaystyle(\mathrm{L}_{h}^{*}(\omega^{\triangleright}_{G})\circ d\mathrm{L}_{h})(v^{\dagger}|_{a})=

\displaystyle\ (\mathrm{L}_{h}^{*}(\omega^{\triangleright}_{G})\circ d\mathrm{L}_{h})(d\mathrm{L}_{a}|_{e}(v))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \omega^{\triangleright}_{G}(d\mathrm{L}_{h\cdot a}|_{e}(v))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \omega_{G}^{\triangleright}(v^{\dagger}|_{h\cdot a})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ 1\otimes v

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \omega^{\triangleright}_{G}(v^{\dagger}|_{a}).

Since
\mathcal{T}_{G}
is generated by the sections in
\Gamma(G,\mathcal{T}_{G})
,
(
70
) implies
the commutativity of the left-hand square of (
–
‣
(i)
).
Moreover, for the same reason as above, the commutativity of the right-hand square of (
–
‣
(i)
) can be verified by the following sequence of equalities:

(71)

\displaystyle(\mathrm{R}_{h}^{*}(\omega_{G}^{\triangleright})\circ d\mathrm{R}_{h})(v^{\dagger}|_{a})=

\displaystyle\ (\mathrm{R}_{h}^{*}(\omega_{G}^{\triangleright})\circ d\mathrm{R}_{h})(d\mathrm{L}_{a}|_{e}(v))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \omega_{G}^{\triangleright}((d\mathrm{R}_{h}|_{a}\circ d\mathrm{L}_{h\cdot a}|_{h^{-1}}\circ d\mathrm{L}_{h^{-1}}|_{e})(v))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \omega_{G}^{\triangleright}((d\mathrm{L}_{h\cdot a}|_{e}\circ d\mathrm{R}_{h}|_{h^{-1}}\circ d\mathrm{L}_{h^{-1}}|_{e})(v))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \omega_{G}^{\triangleright}((d\mathrm{R}_{h}|_{h^{-1}}\circ d\mathrm{L}_{h^{-1}}|_{e}(v))^{\dagger}|_{h\cdot a})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \omega^{\triangleright}_{G}(\mathrm{Ad}_{G}(h^{-1})(v)^{\dagger}|_{h\cdot a})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ 1\otimes\mathrm{Ad}_{G}(h^{-1})(v)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ (\mathrm{id}\otimes\mathrm{Ad}_{G}(h^{-1}))(1\otimes v)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ ((\mathrm{id}\otimes\mathrm{Ad}_{G}(h^{-1}))\circ\omega_{G}^{\triangleright})(v^{\dagger}|_{a}).

Finally, we have

(72)

\displaystyle(g^{*}(\omega_{G^{\prime}}^{\triangleright})\circ dg)(v^{\dagger}|_{a})=

\displaystyle\ (g^{*}(\omega_{G^{\prime}}^{\triangleright})\circ dg)(d\mathrm{L}_{a}|_{e}(v))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \omega_{G^{\prime}}^{\triangleright}((dg|_{a}\circ d\mathrm{L}_{a}|_{e})(v))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \omega_{G^{\prime}}^{\triangleright}((d\mathrm{L}_{g(a)}|_{e}\circ dg|_{e})(v))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \omega_{G^{\prime}}^{\triangleright}(dg|_{e}(v)^{\dagger}|_{g(a)})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ (1\otimes dg|_{e}(v))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ (\mathrm{id}\otimes dg|_{e})(1\otimes v)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ ((\mathrm{id}\otimes dg|_{e})\circ\omega^{\triangleright}_{G})(v^{\dagger}|_{a}).

This proves the commutativity of (
(ii)
), and hence, completes
the proof of this proposition.
∎

Next, let

\mathcal{E}
be a
G
-bundle on
X
.
The identification
\mathfrak{g}=\Gamma(G,\mathcal{T}_{G})^{G}
gives an
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-linear isomorphism

(73)

\displaystyle\omega_{\mathcal{E}/X}^{\triangleright}:\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{E}/X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{E}}\otimes\mathfrak{g}.

Indeed, if
\mathcal{E}=X\times G
(i.e., the trivial
G
-bundle), then
we can define
\omega_{X\times G/T}^{\triangleright}
as the pull-back of
\omega^{\triangleright}_{G}
via the second projection
\mathrm{pr}_{2}:X\times G\rightarrow G
, i.e.,
the composite isomorphism

(74)

\displaystyle\omega_{X\times G/T}^{\triangleright}:\mathcal{T}_{X\times G/X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathrm{pr}_{2}^{*}(\mathcal{T}_{G})\xrightarrow{\mathrm{pr}_{2}^{*}(\omega_{G}^{\triangleright})}\mathrm{pr}_{2}^{*}(\mathcal{O}_{G}\otimes\mathfrak{g})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathcal{O}_{X\times G}\otimes\mathfrak{g},

where the first arrow is obtained by differentiating
\mathrm{pr}_{2}
.
Moreover, suppose that we are given an étale scheme
U
over
X
and an automorphism
of the trivial
G
-bundle
U\times G
;
it may be expressed as
the left-translation by some
U
-rational point of
G
.
The result of Proposition
3.1.1
, (i), implies that

\omega_{U\times G/G}^{\triangleright}
is invariant under pull-back via this automorphism.
Thus, the isomorphisms
\omega_{U\times G/G}^{\triangleright}
defined for various
U
’s equipped with a local trivialization
\mathcal{E}\cong U\times G
of
\mathcal{E}
may be glued together to form a well-defined isomorphism
\omega_{\mathcal{E}/X}^{\triangleright}:\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{E}/X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{E}}\otimes\mathfrak{g}
, as desired.
In particular, we obtain the
\mathfrak{g}
-valued
1
-form

(75)

\displaystyle\omega_{\mathcal{E}/X}\in\Gamma(\mathcal{E},\Omega_{\mathcal{E}/X}\otimes\mathfrak{g})\left(\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{E}}}(\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{E}/X},\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{E}}\otimes\mathfrak{g})\right)

on
\mathcal{E}/X
corresponding to
\omega_{\mathcal{E}/X}^{\triangleright}
.
By construction, we have
\omega_{G/k}=\omega_{G}
.

3.2.
The curvature of a
1
-form

For
\mathfrak{g}
-valued
1
-forms
\omega_{1}
,
\omega_{2}
on
X/S
,
we define
[\omega_{1},\omega_{2}]
to be the
\mathfrak{g}
-valued
2
-form on
X/S
given by

(76)

\displaystyle[\omega_{1},\omega_{2}]^{\triangleright}(\partial_{1},\partial_{2}):=[\omega_{1}^{\triangleright}(\partial_{1}),\omega_{2}^{\triangleright}(\partial_{2})]-[\omega_{1}^{\triangleright}(\partial_{2}),\omega_{2}^{\triangleright}(\partial_{1})]

for any local sections
\partial_{1},\partial_{2}\in\mathcal{T}_{X/S}
.
In particular, for a
\mathfrak{g}
-valued
1
-form
\omega
, we have

[\omega,\omega]^{\triangleright}(\partial_{1},\partial_{2})=2\cdot[\omega^{\triangleright}(\partial_{1}),\omega^{\triangleright}(\partial_{2})]
.
Also, for a
\mathfrak{g}
-valued
1
-form
\omega
on
X/S
,
the
exterior differential
d\omega
of
\omega
is defined as the
\mathfrak{g}
-valued
2
-form on
X/S
given by

(77)

\displaystyle(d\omega)^{\triangleright}(\partial_{1},\partial_{2}):=\partial_{1}(\omega^{\triangleright}(\partial_{2}))-\partial_{2}(\omega^{\triangleright}(\partial_{1}))-\omega^{\triangleright}([\partial_{1},\partial_{2}])

for any local sections
\partial_{1}
,
\partial_{2}\in\mathcal{T}_{X/S}
.
Then,
the
curvature
of a
\mathfrak{g}
-valued
1
-form
\omega
on
X/S
is defined as the
\mathfrak{g}
-valued
2
-form

(78)

\displaystyle\psi_{\omega}:=d\omega+\frac{1}{2}\cdot[\omega,\omega]\in\Gamma(X,(\bigwedge^{2}\Omega_{X/S})\otimes\mathfrak{g}).

That is to say, it is given by the formula

(79)

\displaystyle\psi_{\omega}^{\triangleright}(\partial_{1},\partial_{2})=\partial_{1}(\omega^{\triangleright}(\partial_{2}))-\partial_{2}(\omega^{\triangleright}(\partial_{1}))+[\omega^{\triangleright}(\partial_{1}),\omega^{\triangleright}(\partial_{2})]-\omega^{\triangleright}([\partial_{1},\partial_{2}])

for any local sections
\partial_{1},\partial_{2}\in\mathcal{T}_{X/S}
.
We shall say that
\omega
is
flat
if
\psi_{\omega}=0
.
We know (cf.
[
54
, § 1.1, Proposition 1.3]
) that the Maurer-Cartan form
\omega_{G}
is flat,
i.e.,

(80)

\displaystyle\left(\psi_{\omega_{G}}:=\right)d\omega_{G}+\frac{1}{2}\cdot[\omega_{G},\omega_{G}]=0.

3.3.
Connections and
1
-forms on the trivial bundle

Let us consider the relationship between the notion of curvature defined above and the classical notion of curvature of a connection (cf. (
406
)).
Denote by
\mathrm{pr}_{1}
(resp.,
\mathrm{pr}_{2}
) the first projection
X\times G\rightarrow X
(resp., the second projection
X\times G\rightarrow G
).
In particular,
\mathrm{pr}_{1}
is nothing but the structure morphism of the trivial
G
-bundle
X\times G
on
X
.
The differentials of
\mathrm{pr}_{1}
and
\mathrm{pr}_{2}
give a direct sum decomposition

(81)

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{X\times G/S}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathrm{pr}_{1}^{*}(\mathcal{T}_{X/S})\oplus\mathrm{pr}_{2}^{*}(\mathcal{T}_{G}).

By applying the functor
\mathrm{pr}_{1*}(-)^{G}
to this isomorphism, we obtain
a decomposition

(82)

\displaystyle\tau:\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{X\times G/S}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathcal{T}_{X/S}\oplus\mathcal{O}_{X}\otimes\mathfrak{g}{\@index{\@indexphrase{$\tau$}}}

of
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{X\times G/S}
, where the second factor
\mathcal{O}_{X}\otimes\mathfrak{g}
in the codomain is naturally isomorphic to
\mathfrak{g}_{X\times G}
.
The morphism
d_{X\times G}:\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{X\times G/S}\rightarrow\mathcal{T}_{X/S}
(cf. (
405
)) coincides, via
\tau
, with the first projection
\mathcal{T}_{X/S}\oplus\mathcal{O}_{X}\otimes\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow\mathcal{T}_{X/S}
.
Under the identification given by
\tau
, the Lie bracket operation on
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{X\times G/S}
is given by

(83)

\displaystyle[(\partial_{1},a_{1}\otimes v_{1}),(\partial_{2},a_{2}\otimes v_{2})]=([\partial_{1},\partial_{2}],a_{1}a_{2}\otimes[v_{1},v_{2}]+\partial_{1}(a_{2})\otimes v_{2}-\partial_{2}(a_{1})\otimes v_{1})

for any local sections
\partial_{i}\in\mathcal{T}_{X/S}
,
a_{i}\in\mathcal{O}_{X}
, and any
v_{i}\in\mathfrak{g}
(
i=1,2
).

Now, let
\omega
be a
\mathfrak{g}
-valued
1
-form on
X/S
.
The assignment
\partial\mapsto(\partial,\omega^{\triangleright}(\partial))
for each local section
\partial\in\mathcal{T}_{X/S}
defines, via
\tau
,
an
S
-connection

(84)

\displaystyle\nabla_{\omega}:\mathcal{T}_{X/S}\rightarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{X\times G/S}

on the trivial
G
-bundle
X\times G
.
The connection

\nabla_{0}
(i.e.,
\nabla_{\omega}
with
\omega=0
) is called the
trivial connection
.
By taking account of (
83
),
we can verify the equality

(85)

\displaystyle\psi_{\omega}=\psi_{\nabla_{\omega}}

of elements in
\Gamma(X,(\bigwedge^{2}\Omega_{X/S})\otimes\mathfrak{g})\left(=\Gamma(X,(\bigwedge^{2}\Omega_{X/S})\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\mathfrak{g}_{X\times G})\right)
.
In particular,
\omega
is flat (in the above sense) if and only if
\nabla_{\omega}
is flat (in the sense of §
7.1
).

3.4.
Cartan geometries

We shall recall the definition of a Cartan geometry.
A Cartan geometry has been formulated mainly in the differential (and holomorphic) geometric setting, but it can also be defined for schemes.

Definition 3.4.1
.

(i)

A
Cartan geometry with model
(G,H)
on
X/S
is a pair

(86)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\clubsuit}:=(\mathcal{E}_{H},\omega)

consisting of an
H
-bundle
\mathcal{E}_{H}
on
X
and
a
\mathfrak{g}
-valued
1
-form
\omega
on
\mathcal{E}_{H}/S

such that the corresponding
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{E}_{H}}
-linear morphism
\omega^{\triangleright}:\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{E}_{H}/S}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{E}_{H}}\otimes\mathfrak{g}

satisfies the following conditions:

–

The morphism
\omega^{\triangleright}
is an isomorphism and the following square diagram is commutative:

\textstyle{\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{E}_{H}/X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\scriptstyle{\omega^{\triangleright}_{\mathcal{E}_{H}/X}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{inclusion}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{E}_{H}}\otimes\mathfrak{h}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{inclusion}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{E}_{H}/S}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\scriptstyle{\omega^{\triangleright}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{E}_{H}}\otimes\mathfrak{g}.}

–

For any
h\in H(k)
,
the following square diagram is commutative:

\textstyle{\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{E}_{H}/S}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\omega^{\triangleright}}
\scriptstyle{\wr}
\scriptstyle{d\mathrm{R}_{h}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{E}_{H}}\otimes\mathfrak{g}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\wr}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}\otimes\mathrm{Ad}(h^{-1})}
\textstyle{\mathrm{R}_{h}^{*}(\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{E}_{H}/S})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{R}_{h}^{*}(\omega^{\triangleright})}
\textstyle{\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{E}_{H}}\otimes\mathfrak{g}.}

(ii)

Let
\mathcal{E}^{\clubsuit}:=(\mathcal{E}_{H},\omega)
and
\mathcal{E}^{\prime\clubsuit}:=(\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{H},\omega^{\prime})
be Cartan geometries with model
(G,H)
on
X/S
.
An
isomorphism of Cartan geometries with model
(G,H)
from
\mathcal{E}^{\clubsuit}
to
\mathcal{E}^{\prime\clubsuit}
is defined as an isomorphism
\eta:\mathcal{E}_{H}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{H}
of
H
-bundles with

\eta^{*}(\omega^{\prime})=\omega
.

Definition 3.4.2
.

Let
\mathcal{E}^{\clubsuit}:=(\mathcal{E}_{H},\omega)
be
a Cartan geometry with model
(G,H)
on
X/S
.
Then,
we shall say that
(\mathcal{E}_{H},\omega)
is
flat
if
\omega
is flat.

Denote by

(95)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Car}_{(G,H),X/S}\ \left(\text{resp.,}\ \mathrm{Car}^{\mathrm{flat}}_{(G,H),X/S}\right)

the set of isomorphism classes of Cartan geometries (resp., flat Cartan geometries) with model
(G,H)
on
X/S
.

Example 3.4.3

(Standard example)
.

Let us recall the standard (flat) Cartan geometry on the
S
-scheme
P_{S}
(cf. (
27
)).
We shall write
\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{triv}}_{H}:=S\times G
; it may be regarded as an
H
-bundle on
P_{S}
by putting the morphism
\mathrm{id}_{S}\times\pi_{G}:\mathcal{E}_{H}^{\mathrm{triv}}\twoheadrightarrow P_{S}
as the structure morphism of
\mathcal{E}_{H}^{\mathrm{triv}}
over
P_{S}
.
Note that
\mathcal{E}_{H}^{\mathrm{triv}}
specifies an
H
-reduction on the trivial
G
-bundle
P_{S}\times G
on
P_{S}
via the morphism

(96)

\displaystyle(\mathrm{id}_{S}\times\pi_{G},\mathrm{pr}_{2}):\mathcal{E}_{H}^{\mathrm{triv}}\left(=S\times G\right)\rightarrow P_{S}\times G,

where
\mathrm{pr}_{2}
denotes the second projection
S\times G\rightarrow G
.
Then, it follows from Proposition
3.1.1
, (i), and (
80
) that
the pair

(97)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{triv},\clubsuit}:=(\mathcal{E}_{H}^{\mathrm{triv}},\omega_{S\times G/S})

forms a flat Cartan geometry with model
(G,H)
on
P_{S}/S
.

3.5.
Pull-back and base-change of Cartan geometries

Let
Y
and
y
be as in the former half of §
2.4
.
Also, let
\mathcal{E}^{\clubsuit}:=(\mathcal{E}_{H},\omega)
be a Cartan geometry with model
(G,H)
on
X/S
.
The base-change
y^{*}(\mathcal{E}_{H})
of
\mathcal{E}_{H}
by
y
defines an
H
-bundle on
Y
.
If
y_{\mathcal{E}_{H}}
denotes the projection
y^{*}(\mathcal{E}_{H})\rightarrow\mathcal{E}_{H}
,
then its differential
dy_{\mathcal{E}_{H}}:\mathcal{T}_{y^{*}(\mathcal{E}_{H})/S}\rightarrow y_{\mathcal{E}_{H}}^{*}(\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{E}_{H}/S})

is an isomorphism because of the étaleness of
y
.
Hence, the pull-back
y^{*}(\omega):=y_{\mathcal{E}_{H}}^{*}(\omega)

specifies a
\mathfrak{g}
-valued
1
-form on
y^{*}(\mathcal{E}_{H})/S
.
One may verify that
the pair

(98)

\displaystyle y^{*}(\mathcal{E}^{\clubsuit}):=(y^{*}(\mathcal{E}_{H}),y^{*}(\omega))

forms a Cartan geometry with model
(G,H)
on
Y/S
.
Moreover, the equality
y^{*}_{\mathcal{E}_{H}}(\psi_{\omega})=\psi_{y^{*}(\omega)}
holds under the identification
\mathcal{T}_{y^{*}(\mathcal{E}_{H})/S}=y^{*}_{\mathcal{E}_{H}}(\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{E}_{H}/S})
given by
dy_{\mathcal{E}_{H}}
, so

y^{*}(\mathcal{E}^{\clubsuit})
is flat if
\mathcal{E}^{\clubsuit}
is flat.
The Cartan geometry
y^{*}(\mathcal{E}^{\clubsuit})
is called the
pull-back
of
\mathcal{E}^{\clubsuit}
. The resulting assignment

\mathcal{E}^{\clubsuit}\mapsto y^{*}(\mathcal{E}^{\clubsuit})

defines maps

(99)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Car}_{(G,H),X/S}\rightarrow\mathrm{Car}_{(G,H),Y/S},\hskip 14.22636pt\mathrm{Car}_{(G,H),X/S}^{\mathrm{flat}}\rightarrow\mathrm{Car}_{(G,H),Y/S}^{\mathrm{flat}}.

Note that the formation of a (flat) Cartan geometry with model
(G,H)
on
X/S
has descent with respect to the étale topology on
X
.

Next,
let
s:S^{\prime}\rightarrow S
and
X^{\prime}
be as in the latter half of §
2.4
, and let
\mathcal{E}^{\clubsuit}
be as above.
The base-change
s^{*}(\mathcal{E}_{H})
defines an
H
-bundle on
X^{\prime}
.
Since the differential
ds_{\mathcal{E}_{H}}:\mathcal{T}_{s^{*}(\mathcal{E}_{H})/S^{\prime}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\rightarrow}}s_{\mathcal{E}_{H}}^{*}(\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{E}_{H}/S})
of the natural projection
s_{\mathcal{E}_{H}}:s^{*}(\mathcal{E}_{H})\rightarrow\mathcal{E}_{H}
is an isomorphism,
the base-change
s^{*}(\omega)
of
\omega
defines a
\mathfrak{g}
-valued
1
-form on
s^{*}(\mathcal{E}_{H})
.
Moreover, the pair

(100)

\displaystyle s^{*}(\mathcal{E}^{\clubsuit}):=(s^{*}(\mathcal{E}_{H}),s^{*}(\omega))

forms a Cartan geometry with model
(G,H)
on
X^{\prime}/S^{\prime}
.
Under the identification
\mathcal{T}_{s^{*}(\mathcal{E}_{H})/S^{\prime}}=s_{\mathcal{E}_{H}}^{*}(\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{E}_{H}/S})
given by
ds_{\mathcal{E}_{H}}
, the equality
s^{*}_{\mathcal{E}_{H}}(\psi_{\omega})=\psi_{s^{*}(\omega)}
holds.
This implies that
s^{*}(\mathcal{E}^{\clubsuit})
is flat if
\mathcal{E}^{\clubsuit}
is flat.
The Cartan geometry
s^{*}(\mathcal{E}^{\clubsuit})
is called the
base-change
of
\mathcal{E}^{\clubsuit}
, and
the assignment

\mathcal{E}^{\clubsuit}\mapsto s^{*}(\mathcal{E}^{\clubsuit})

defines maps

(101)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Car}_{(G,H),X/S}\rightarrow\mathrm{Car}_{(G,H),X^{\prime}/S^{\prime}},\hskip 14.22636pt\mathrm{Car}_{(G,H),X/S}^{\mathrm{flat}}\rightarrow\mathrm{Car}_{(G,H),X^{\prime}/S^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{flat}}.

3.6.
From Frobenius-Ehresmann structures to Cartan geometries

In this subsection, we construct a Cartan geometry with model
(G,H)
using an
F
-
(G,H)
-structure.

Let
\mathcal{S}^{\heartsuit}
be an
F
-
(G,H)
-structure on
X/S
.
Since
G
is quasi-projective (cf.
[
38
, § 8, Theorem 8.43]
),
we obtain the
right

G
-bundle
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{S}^{\heartsuit},G}
on
X
representing the inverse of the left
G_{X}
-torsor
\mathcal{S}^{\heartsuit}\times^{G_{X/S}^{(1)}}G_{X}
(cf.
[
35
, Chap. III, § 4, Theorem 4.3]
).
The graphs
\Gamma_{\phi}:U\rightarrow U\times P\left(=U\times_{S}P_{S}\right)
for various local sections
\phi:U\rightarrow P_{S}
in
\mathcal{S}^{\heartsuit}

may be glued together to construct
an
H
-reduction
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{S}^{\heartsuit},H}
of
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{S}^{\heartsuit},G}
.
